One Energy Game for the Spectrum between Branching Bisimilarity and Weak Trace Semantics
Benjamin Bisping, David N. Jansen

Abstract
We provide the first generalized game characterization of van Glabbeek’s linear-time–branching-time spectrum with silent steps. Thereby, one multi-dimensional energy game can be used to characterize and decide a wide array of weak behavioral equivalences between stability-respecting branching bisimilarity and weak trace equivalence in one go. To establish correctness, we relate attacker-winning energy budgets and distinguishing sublanguages of Hennessy–Milner logic that we characterize by eight dimensions of formula expressiveness.



1
Introduction: Mechanizing the Spectrum

Picking the right notion of behavioral equivalence for a particular use case can be hard.
1
1

1

Some accounts of researchers who struggled to pick fitting equivalence for verification and encoding challenges:
[
3
,
4
,
26
]
.

Theoretically, van Glabbeek’s “linear-time–branching-time spectrum”
[
23
,
24
,
25
]
brings order to the zoo of equivalences by casting them as a hierarchy of modal logics.
But practically, it is difficult to navigate in particular the second part
[
24
]
, which considers so-called
weak equivalences
that abstract from “internal” behavior, expressed by “silent”
\tau
-steps.
Abstracting internal behavior is crucial to model communication happening without participation of the observer and refinements,
that is, for virtually every application.

In this paper, we show how to
operationalize the silent-step linear-time–branching-time spectrum
of
[
24
]
.
We enable researchers to provide a set of processes that ought to be equated (or distinguished) for their scenario and to learn “where” in the spectrum this set of (in-)equivalences holds.
In prior work on the strong spectrum
[
23
]
(without silent steps), we dubbed this process
linear-time–branching-time spectroscopy

[
8
]
.
Implicitly, we obtain decision procedures (and games) for each individual notion of equivalence as a by-product.

As outlined in
Figure 1
, we apply our recent approach
[
8
,
6
]
to use a
generalized bisimulation game

with moves corresponding to sets of conceivable distinguishing formulas.
The background is that
formulas can be partially ordered by the amount of Hennessy–Milner logic expressiveness
they use in a way that aligns with the spectrum.
The game can then be understood as a
multi-weighted energy game

[
6
,
14
,
27
]
where moves use up attacker’s resources to distinguish processes.
So, defender-won energy levels reveal non-distinguishing subsets of Hennessy–Milner logic (HML) and thus sets of maintained equivalences.

Applying the above approach to the weak spectrum faces many obstacles:
The modal logics of the weak spectrum in
[
24
]
are quite intricate and are not closed under HML-subterms.
Also, van Glabbeek
[
24
]
does not account for unstable linear-time equivalences, but other publications like Gazda et al.
[
20
]
use these.
On the game side, existing weak bisimulation games by De Frutos Escrig et al.
[
19
]
and Bisping et al.
[
10
]
lack moves for many observations that are relevant for weaker notions in the spectrum.
This paper shows how all this can still be brought together.

p
is preordered to
q
w.r.t. notion of
equivalence
N
:\!\iff
van Glabbeek’s
spectrum approach
[
24
]
no formula below
e_{N}
distinguishes
p
from
q
(
Section 2
)
\iff
Bisping’s spectroscopy
approach
[
6
]
(
Section 4
)
defender wins spectroscopy game
\mathcal{G}_{\vartriangle}
from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}[}p,\{q\}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}]_{\mathtt{a}}^{\smash{\scriptscriptstyle}}}
with energy
e_{N}
(
Section 3
)

Figure 1:
How the paper combines the weak spectrum
[
24
]
and the spectroscopy approach
[
6
]
.

Contributions.

At its core, this paper extends the spectroscopy energy game of
[
6
]
by modalities needed to cover the weak equivalence spectrum of
[
24
]
, namely, delayed observations, stable conjunctions, and branching conjunctions.
More precisely:

•

In
Section 2
, we capture a big chunk of the
linear-time–branching-time spectrum with silent steps by measuring expressive powers
used in an HML-subset, which we prove to correspond to stability-respecting branching bisimilarity.

•

In
Section 3
, we introduce the first generalized game characterization of the silent-step equivalence spectrum. For this, we adapt the
spectroscopy energy game
of
[
6
]
to account for distinctions in terms of delayed observations (
\langle\varepsilon\rangle\langle a\rangle\ldots
), stable conjunctions (
\langle\varepsilon\rangle{\bigwedge}\{\neg\langle\tau\rangle\mathsf{T},\ldots\}
), and branching conjunctions (
\langle\varepsilon\rangle{\bigwedge}\{\langle a\rangle\ldots,\langle\varepsilon\rangle\ldots\}
).

•

Section 4
proves that
winning energy levels and equivalences coincide
by closely relating distinguishing formulas and ways the attacker may win the energy game.
The proofs have been Isabelle/HOL-formalized.

•

Section 5
lays out how to use the game to
decide all equivalences at once
in exponential time using our prototype tool for everyday research.

Distinctions and Equivalences in Systems with Silent Steps

This paper follows the paradigm that
equivalence is the absence of possibilities to distinguish
.
Equivalently, one could speak about apartness, i.e. the view that non-equivalence is based on evidence of difference
[
21
]
.
We begin by introducing
distinguishing Hennessy–Milner logic formulas (
Subsection 2.1
),
and a quantitative characterization of weak equivalences in terms of distinctive capabilities (
Subsection 2.2
).

2.1
Transition Systems and Hennessy–Milner Logic

Definition 2.1

(Labeled transition system with silent steps)
.

A
labeled transition system
is a tuple
\mathcal{S}=(\mathcal{P},\Sigma,\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\smash{}}})
where
\mathcal{P}
is the set of
processes,

\Sigma
is the set of
actions,
and
{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\smash{}}}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}\times\Sigma\times\mathcal{P}
is the
transition relation
.

\tau\in\Sigma
labels
silent steps
and
\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}
is notation for the reflexive transitive closure of
internal activity

\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\smash{\tau}}}^{*}
.
The name
\varepsilon\notin\Sigma
is reserved and indicates no (visible) action.
A process
p
is called
stable
if
p\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 5.11302pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\smash{\tau}}}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 5.11302pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\smash{\tau}}}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.97311pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\smash{\tau}}}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.97311pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\smash{\tau}}}}}}
.
We write
p\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\smash{\text{\rm(}\alpha\text{\rm)}}}}p^{\prime}
if
p\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\smash{\alpha}}}p^{\prime}
, or if
\alpha=\tau
and
p=p^{\prime}
.

We implicitly lift the relations to sets of processes
P\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\smash{\alpha}}}P^{\prime}
(with
P,P^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
,
\alpha\in\Sigma
), which is defined to be true if
P^{\prime}=\{p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}\mid\exists p\in P\ldotp p\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\smash{\alpha}}}p^{\prime}\}
.

Example 2.1
.

Figure 2
presents transition systems of four processes:

\mathsf{P_{e}}
makes a nondeterministic choice
\mathit{op}
between
\mathit{a}
and
\mathit{b}
, performing arbitrarily many
\mathit{idle}
-actions in between.

\mathsf{P_{\ell}}
does the same but can change the choice while idling.

\mathsf{P^{\tau}_{e}}
and
\mathsf{P^{\tau}_{\ell}}
are variants of the two obtained by abstracting
\mathit{idle}
into
\tau
-actions.

The example is helpful to test whether a process equivalence can be a congruence for abstraction.
Any congruence for abstraction
\sim
would need to have the property that
\mathsf{P_{e}}\sim\mathsf{P_{\ell}}
implies
\mathsf{P^{\tau}_{e}}\sim\mathsf{P^{\tau}_{\ell}}
.
So, if we just had a quick way of testing for all weak behavioral equivalences at once, we could quickly narrow down which equivalences work for this example.
Using this paper’s spectroscopy algorithm, we can achieve this.

Bisimilarity and other notions of equivalence can conveniently by defined in terms of Hennessy–Milner logic.
We direct our attention to variants that allow for silent behavior to happen before visible actions are observed.
We thus focus on the following variant,
where the
brick-red
part represents
stable conjunctions

and the
steel-blue
part
branching conjunctions
:

Definition 2.2

(Branching Hennessy–Milner logic)
.

We define
stability-respecting branching
Hennessy–Milner modal logic,

\mathsf{HML}_{\mathrm{srbb}}
, over an alphabet of actions
\Sigma
by the following context-free grammar starting with
\varphi
:

\begin{array}[]{c@{\;\displaystyle}llr}\varphi{}::=&\langle\varepsilon\rangle\chi&&\text{“delayed observation”}\\
|&{\bigwedge}\{\psi,\psi,...\}&&\text{“immediate conjunction”}\\
\chi{}::=&\langle a\rangle\varphi&\quad\text{with }a\in\Sigma\setminus\{\tau\}&\text{“observation”}\\
|&{\bigwedge}\{\psi,\psi,...\}&&\text{“standard conjunction”}\\
|&{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}{\bigwedge}\{\neg\langle\tau\rangle\mathsf{T},\psi,\psi,...\}}&&{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\text{“stable conjunction”}}\\
|&{\color[rgb]{0,0.7,0.7}{\bigwedge}\{\text{\rm(}\alpha\text{\rm)}\varphi,\psi,\psi,...\}}&\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0.7,0.7}\text{with }\alpha\in\Sigma}&{\color[rgb]{0,0.7,0.7}\text{“branching conjunction”}}\\
\psi{}::=&\neg\langle\varepsilon\rangle\chi\mid\langle\varepsilon\rangle\chi&&\text{“negative / positive conjuncts”}\end{array}

Its semantics
\;\smash{{\llbracket\;\cdot\;\rrbracket^{\mathcal{S}}}}\colon\mathsf{HML}_{\mathrm{srbb}}\to\mathbf{2}^{\mathcal{P}}\!
,
where a formula “is true,”
over a transition system
\mathcal{S}=(\mathcal{P},\Sigma,\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\smash{}}})

is defined in mutual recursion with helper functions
{\llbracket\;\cdot\;\rrbracket_{\varepsilon}}
for subformulas in the “delayed” context (
\chi
-productions) and
{\llbracket\;\cdot\;\rrbracket_{\wedge}}
for conjuncts (
\psi
-productions):

\displaystyle{\llbracket\langle\varepsilon\rangle\chi\rrbracket^{\mathcal{S}}}\mathrel{\coloneqq}

\displaystyle\;\{p\in\mathcal{P}\mid\exists p^{\prime}\in{\llbracket\chi\rrbracket^{\mathcal{S}}_{\varepsilon}}\ldotp p\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}p^{\prime}\}

\displaystyle\smash{{\llbracket{\bigwedge}\Psi\rrbracket^{\mathcal{S}}}}\mathrel{\coloneqq}\smash{{\llbracket{\bigwedge}\Psi\rrbracket^{\mathcal{S}}_{\varepsilon}}}\mathrel{\coloneqq}

\displaystyle\;\bigcap\{{\llbracket\psi\rrbracket^{\mathcal{S}}_{\wedge}}\mid\psi\in\Psi\}

\displaystyle{\llbracket\langle a\rangle\varphi\rrbracket^{\mathcal{S}}_{\varepsilon}}\mathrel{\coloneqq}

\displaystyle\;\{p\in\mathcal{P}\mid\exists p^{\prime}\in{\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket^{\mathcal{S}}}\ldotp p\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\smash{a}}}p^{\prime}\}

\displaystyle{\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\neg\langle\tau\rangle\mathsf{T}}\rrbracket^{\mathcal{S}}_{\wedge}}\mathrel{\coloneqq}

\displaystyle\;\{p\in\mathcal{P}\mid p\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 5.11302pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\smash{\tau}}}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 5.11302pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\smash{\tau}}}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.97311pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\smash{\tau}}}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.97311pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\smash{\tau}}}}}}\}

\displaystyle{\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.7,0.7}\text{\rm(}\alpha\text{\rm)}\varphi}\rrbracket^{\mathcal{S}}_{\wedge}}\mathrel{\coloneqq}

\displaystyle\;\{p\in\mathcal{P}\mid\exists p^{\prime}\in{\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket^{\mathcal{S}}}\ldotp p\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\smash{\text{\rm(}\alpha\text{\rm)}}}}p^{\prime}\}

\displaystyle{\llbracket\neg\langle\varepsilon\rangle\chi\rrbracket^{\mathcal{S}}_{\wedge}}\mathrel{\coloneqq}

\displaystyle\;\mathcal{P}\setminus{\llbracket\langle\varepsilon\rangle\chi\rrbracket^{\mathcal{S}}}

\displaystyle{\llbracket\langle\varepsilon\rangle\chi\rrbracket^{\mathcal{S}}_{\wedge}}\mathrel{\coloneqq}

\displaystyle\;{\llbracket\langle\varepsilon\rangle\chi\rrbracket^{\mathcal{S}}}

{\bigwedge}\{\psi,\psi,...\}
in the grammar stands for conjunction with arbitrary branching.
We write
\mathsf{T}
for the empty conjunction
{\bigwedge}\varnothing
.

Definition 2.3

(Distinguishing formulas and preordering languages)
.

A formula
\varphi\in\mathsf{HML}_{\mathrm{srbb}}
is said to
distinguish
a process
p
from
q
iff
p\in{\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket^{\smash{\mathcal{S}}}}
and
q\notin\smash{{\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket^{\smash{\mathcal{S}}}}}
​.
The formula is said to
distinguish
a process
p
from a set of processes
Q
iff it is true for
p
and false for every
q\in Q
.

A sublogic,
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{\mathit{N}}}\subseteq\mathsf{HML}_{\mathrm{srbb}}
, corresponding to a notion of observability
N
,
distinguishes
two processes,
p\not\preceq_{\mathrm{\mathit{N}}}q
, if there is
\varphi\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{\mathit{N}}}
with
p\in{\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket^{\smash{\mathcal{S}}}}
and
q\notin\smash{{\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket^{\smash{\mathcal{S}}}}}
. Otherwise
N

preorders
them,
p\preceq_{\mathrm{\mathit{N}}}q
. If processes are mutually
N
-preordered,
p\preceq_{\mathrm{\mathit{N}}}q
and
q\preceq_{\mathrm{\mathit{N}}}p
, then they are considered
N\!
-
equivalent
,
p\sim_{\mathrm{\mathit{N}}}q
.

Example 2.2
.

In
Example 2.1
,
\varphi_{\tau}\mathrel{\coloneqq}\langle\varepsilon\rangle\langle op\rangle\langle\varepsilon\rangle{\bigwedge}\{\neg\langle\varepsilon\rangle\langle b\rangle\mathsf{T}\}
distinguishes
\mathsf{P^{\tau}_{e}}
from
\mathsf{P^{\tau}_{\ell}}
.

\varphi_{\tau}
states that a weak
\mathit{op}
-step may happen such that, afterwards,
\mathit{b}
is not
\tau
-reachable.
This is true of
\mathsf{P^{\tau}_{e}}
because of the
\mathsf{A^{\tau}_{e}}
-state, but not of
\mathsf{P^{\tau}_{\ell}}
.

Remark 2.1
.

Definition 2.2
is constructed to fit the distinctive powers we need from HML to characterize varying notions of the weak spectrum by controlling which productions are used.
Subformulas in the grammar usually start with
\langle\varepsilon\rangle\ldots
,
effectively hiding silent steps.
Formulas with fewer
\langle\varepsilon\rangle
-positions bring in additional distinctive power.
We will use immediate conjunctions to distinguish non-delay-bisimilar processes, and branching conjunctions (that contain one positive conjunct without leading
\langle\varepsilon\rangle
) to distinguish non-
\eta
-(bi)similar processes.
Allowing the observation of stabilization,
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\neg\langle\tau\rangle\mathsf{T}}
, increases distinctive power; requiring stabilization for conjunct observations decreases it.

The name already alludes to
\mathsf{HML}_{\mathrm{srbb}}
as a whole characterizing stability-respecting branching bisimilarity.
Let us quickly recall the operational definition for branching bisimilarity (for instance from
[
16
]
):

Definition 2.4

(Branching bisimilarity, operationally)
.

A symmetric relation
\mathcal{R}
is a
branching bisimulation

if, for all
(p,q)\in\mathcal{R}
, a step
p\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\smash{\alpha}}}p^{\prime}
implies (1)
\alpha=\tau
and
(p^{\prime},q)\in\mathcal{R}
, or (2)
q\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}q^{\prime}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\smash{\alpha}}}q^{\prime\prime}
for some
q^{\prime},q^{\prime\prime}
with
(p,q^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}
and
(p^{\prime},q^{\prime\prime})\in\mathcal{R}
.

If moreover every
(p,q)\in\mathcal{R}
with
p\mathrel{\smash{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 5.11302pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\xrightarrow{\tau}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 5.11302pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\xrightarrow{\tau}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.97311pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\xrightarrow{\tau}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.97311pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\xrightarrow{\tau}}}}}}
implies that there is some
q^{\prime}
with
q\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}q^{\prime}\mathrel{\smash{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 5.11302pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\xrightarrow{\tau}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 5.11302pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\xrightarrow{\tau}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.97311pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\xrightarrow{\tau}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.97311pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\xrightarrow{\tau}}}}}}
and
(p,q^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}
, the relation is
stability-respecting
.

If there is a stability-respecting branching bisimulation
\mathcal{R}_{BB^{sr}}
with
(p_{0},q_{0})\in\mathcal{R}_{BB^{sr}}
, then
p_{0}
and
q_{0}
are stability-respecting branching bisimilar.

The power of
Definition 2.2
to distinguish matches exactly the power of
Definition 2.4
to equate:

silent-step-spectroscopy˙express-pratenddefaultcategory.tex

Lemma 2.1
.

\mathsf{HML}_{\mathrm{srbb}}
characterizes stability-respecting branching bisimilarity.

silent-step-spectroscopy˙express-pratenddefaultcategory.tex

Proof.

We use the standard approach for Hennessy–Milner theorems:
We prove that
\mathcal{R}_{srbb}\mathrel{\coloneqq}\{(p,q)\mid\forall\varphi\in\mathsf{HML}_{\mathrm{srbb}}\ldotp p\in{\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket}\longrightarrow q\in{\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket}\}
is a stability-respecting branching bisimulation by definition,
and that any formula
\varphi\in\mathsf{HML}_{\mathrm{srbb}}
is equally true for stability-respecting branching bisimilar states by induction on the structure of
\varphi
.

Full proof in report
[
7
]
.

∎

Hierarchy of weak behavioral equivalences/preorders, becoming finer towards the top. Each notion
N

.

2.2
Price Spectra of Behavioral Equivalences

Van Glabbeek
[
24
]
uses about 20 binary dimensions to characterize 155 “notions of observability” (derived from five dimensions of testing scenarios).
These then entail behavioral preorders and equivalences given as modal characterizations.
In this subsection, we recast the
notions of observability as coordinates
in a (more quantitative) 8-dimensional space of HML formula expressiveness.

We will “price” formulas of
\mathsf{HML}_{\mathrm{srbb}}
by vectors we call
energies
. The pricing allows to conveniently select subsets of
\mathsf{HML}_{\mathrm{srbb}}
in terms of coordinates.

Definition 2.5

(Energies)
.

We denote as
energies,

\mathbf{En}_{\infty}
, the set
(\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\})^{8}
.

We compare energies component-wise:
({e}_{1},\ldots,{e}_{8})\leq({f}_{1},\ldots,{f}_{8})
iff
e_{i}\leq f_{i}
for each
i
.
Least upper bounds
\sup
are defined as usual as component-wise supremum.

We write
\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{i}
for the standard unit vector where the
i
-th component is
1
and every other component equals
0
.
\mathbf{0}
is defined to be the vector
(0,0,\ldots,0)
. Vector addition and subtraction happen component-wise as usual.

In
Figure 3
, we order weak equivalences along dimensions of
\mathsf{HML}_{\mathrm{srbb}}
-expressiveness in terms of
operator depths
(
i.e.
maximal occurrences of an operator on a path from root to leaf in the abstract syntax tree).
Intuitively, the dimensions are:

1.

Modal depth (of observations
\langle\alpha\rangle
,
\text{\rm(}\alpha\text{\rm)}
),

2.

Depth of
branching conjunctions
(with one observation conjunct not starting with
\langle\varepsilon\rangle
),

3.

Depth of unstable conjunctions (that do not enforce stability by a
\neg\langle\tau\rangle\mathsf{T}
-conjunct),

4.

Depth of
stable conjunctions
(that do enforce stability by a
\neg\langle\tau\rangle\mathsf{T}
-conjunct),

5.

Depth of immediate conjunctions (that are not preceded by
\langle\varepsilon\rangle
),

6.

Maximal modal depth of positive conjuncts in conjunctions,

7.

Maximal modal depth of negative conjuncts in conjunctions,

8.

Depth of negations.

Definition 2.6

(Formula prices)
.

The
expressiveness price
of a formula
\operatorname{\mathsf{expr}}\colon\mathsf{HML}_{\mathrm{srbb}}\rightarrow\mathbf{En}_{\infty}

is defined in mutual recursion with helper functions
\operatorname{\mathsf{expr}}^{\varepsilon}
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{expr}}^{\wedge}
;
if multiple rules apply to a subformula, pick the first one:

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{expr}}\left(\mathsf{T}\right)\mathrel{\coloneqq}\operatorname{\mathsf{expr}}^{\varepsilon}\left(\mathsf{T}\right)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\coloneqq}\mathbf{0}

\displaystyle\textstyle\operatorname{\mathsf{expr}}\left(\langle\varepsilon\rangle\chi\right)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\coloneqq}\textstyle\operatorname{\mathsf{expr}}^{\varepsilon}\left(\chi\right)

\displaystyle\textstyle\operatorname{\mathsf{expr}}\left({\bigwedge}\Psi\right)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\coloneqq}\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{5}+\textstyle\operatorname{\mathsf{expr}}^{\varepsilon}\left({\bigwedge}\Psi\right)

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{expr}}^{\varepsilon}\left(\langle a\rangle\varphi\right)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\coloneqq}\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{1}+\operatorname{\mathsf{expr}}\left(\varphi\right)

\displaystyle\textstyle\operatorname{\mathsf{expr}}^{\varepsilon}\left({\bigwedge}\Psi\right)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\coloneqq}\sup\;\{\operatorname{\mathsf{expr}}^{\wedge}\left(\psi\right)\mid\psi\in\Psi\}+\begin{cases}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{4}}&\text{if }{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\neg\langle\tau\rangle\mathsf{T}}\in\Psi\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0.7,0.7}\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{2}}+\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{3}&\text{if there is }{\color[rgb]{0,0.7,0.7}\text{\rm(}\alpha\text{\rm)}\varphi}\in\Psi\\
\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{3}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{expr}}^{\wedge}\left({\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\neg\langle\tau\rangle\mathsf{T}}\right)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\coloneqq}\mathbf{0}

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{expr}}^{\wedge}\left(\neg\varphi\right)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\coloneqq}\sup\;\{\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{8}+\operatorname{\mathsf{expr}}\left(\varphi\right),\quad(0,0,0,0,0,0,\left(\operatorname{\mathsf{expr}}\left(\varphi\right)\right)_{1},0)\}

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{expr}}^{\wedge}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.7,0.7}\text{\rm(}\alpha\text{\rm)}\varphi}\right)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\coloneqq}\sup\;\{\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{1}+\operatorname{\mathsf{expr}}\left(\varphi\right),\quad(0,0,0,0,0,1+\left(\operatorname{\mathsf{expr}}\left(\varphi\right)\right)_{1},0,0)\}

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{expr}}^{\wedge}\left(\varphi\right)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\coloneqq}\sup\;\{\hphantom{\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{8}+{}}\operatorname{\mathsf{expr}}\left(\varphi\right),\quad(0,0,0,0,0,\left(\operatorname{\mathsf{expr}}\left(\varphi\right)\right)_{1},0,0)\}

Definition 2.7

(Linear-time–branching-time equivalences)
.

Each notion
N
named in Figure
3
with coordinate
e_{N}
is defined through the language of formulas with prices below, i.e., through
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{\mathit{N}}}=\{\varphi\mid\operatorname{\mathsf{expr}}(\varphi)\leq e_{N}\}
.

Recalling
Definition 2.3
, that is,
p\preceq_{\mathrm{\mathit{N}}}q
with respect to notion
N
, iff no
\varphi
with
\operatorname{\mathsf{expr}}(\varphi)\leq e_{N}
distinguishes
p
from
q
.
So, this paper sees notions of preorder / equivalence to be defined through these coordinates and not through other characterizations.

Example 2.3
.

The formula
\varphi_{\tau}=\langle\varepsilon\rangle\langle op\rangle\langle\varepsilon\rangle{\bigwedge}\{\neg\langle\varepsilon\rangle\langle b\rangle\mathsf{T}\}

in
Example 2.2
has expressiveness price
\operatorname{\mathsf{expr}}(\varphi_{\tau})=(2,0,1,0,0,0,1,1).

The coordinate is below the one of failures
e_{\mathrm{F}}=(\infty,0,1,0,0,0,1,1)
in
Figure 3
.
Accordingly,
\mathsf{P^{\tau}_{e}}
is distinguished from
\mathsf{P^{\tau}_{\ell}}
by failure
\varphi_{\tau}\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{F}}
, that is,
\mathsf{P^{\tau}_{e}}\not\preceq_{\mathrm{F}}\mathsf{P^{\tau}_{\ell}}
.
There neither are strictly-stable nor strictly-positive formulas to distinguish
\mathsf{P^{\tau}_{e}}
from
\mathsf{P^{\tau}_{\ell}}
. Therefore, stable bisimulation preorder,
\mathsf{P^{\tau}_{e}}\preceq_{\mathrm{SB}}\mathsf{P^{\tau}_{\ell}}
, and
\eta
-simulation preorder,
\mathsf{P^{\tau}_{e}}\preceq_{\mathrm{\eta S}}\mathsf{P^{\tau}_{\ell}}
, apply.
(The latter implies the more well-known weak simulation preorder.)

For stability-respecting branching bisimilarity, where
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{\mathrm{BB^{sr}}}}=\mathsf{HML}_{\mathrm{srbb}}
,
Lemma 2.1
establishes that our modal characterization corresponds to the common relational definition.
For some notions, there are superficial differences to other modal characterizations in the literature, which do not change distinctive power.
We give two examples.

Example 2.4

(Weak trace equivalence and inclusion)
.

The notion of weak trace inclusion (and equivalence) is defined through
e_{\mathrm{T}}=(\infty,0,0,0,0,0,0,0)
and
Definition 2.6
inducing
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{T}}
, the language given by the grammar:

\displaystyle\varphi_{\mathrm{T}}\quad::=\quad

\displaystyle\quad\langle\varepsilon\rangle\langle a\rangle\varphi_{\mathrm{T}}\quad|\quad\langle\varepsilon\rangle\mathsf{T}\quad|\quad\mathsf{T}.

This slightly deviates from languages one would find in other publications. For instance, Gazda et al.
[
20
]
do not have the second production. But this production does not increase expressiveness, as
{\llbracket\langle\varepsilon\rangle\mathsf{T}\rrbracket}={\llbracket\mathsf{T}\rrbracket}=\mathcal{P}
.

Example 2.5

(Weak bisimulation equivalence and preorder)
.

The logic of weak bisimulation observations
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{B}}
defined through
e_{\mathrm{B}}=(\infty,0,\infty,0,0,\infty,\infty,\infty)
equals the language defined by the grammar:

\displaystyle\varphi_{\mathrm{B}}\quad::=\quad

\displaystyle\langle\varepsilon\rangle\langle a\rangle\varphi_{\mathrm{B}}\quad|\quad\langle\varepsilon\rangle{\bigwedge}\{\psi_{\mathrm{B}},\psi_{\mathrm{B}},\ldots\}\quad|\quad\mathsf{T}

\displaystyle\psi_{\mathrm{B}}\quad::=\quad

\displaystyle\neg\langle\varepsilon\rangle\langle a\rangle\varphi_{\mathrm{B}}\quad|\quad\neg\langle\varepsilon\rangle{\bigwedge}\{\psi_{\mathrm{B}},\psi_{\mathrm{B}},\ldots\}\quad|\quad\langle\varepsilon\rangle\langle a\rangle\varphi_{\mathrm{B}}\quad|\quad\langle\varepsilon\rangle{\bigwedge}\{\psi_{\mathrm{B}},\psi_{\mathrm{B}},\ldots\}.

Let us contrast this to the definition for weak bisimulation observations
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{B^{\prime}}}
from Gazda et al.
[
20
]
:

\displaystyle\varphi_{\mathrm{B^{\prime}}}\quad::=\quad\langle\varepsilon\rangle\varphi_{\mathrm{B^{\prime}}}\quad|\quad\langle\varepsilon\rangle\langle a\rangle\langle\varepsilon\rangle\varphi_{\mathrm{B^{\prime}}}\quad|\quad{\bigwedge}\{\varphi_{\mathrm{B^{\prime}}},\varphi_{\mathrm{B^{\prime}}},\ldots\}\quad|\quad\neg\varphi_{\mathrm{B^{\prime}}}.

Our
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{B}}
allows a few formulas that
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{B^{\prime}}}
lacks, e.g.
\langle\varepsilon\rangle\langle a\rangle\langle\varepsilon\rangle\langle a\rangle\langle\varepsilon\rangle\mathsf{T}
.
This does not add expressiveness as
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{B^{\prime}}}
has
\langle\varepsilon\rangle\langle a\rangle\langle\varepsilon\rangle\langle\varepsilon\rangle\langle a\rangle\langle\varepsilon\rangle\mathsf{T}
and
{\llbracket\langle\varepsilon\rangle\langle\varepsilon\rangle\varphi\rrbracket}={\llbracket\langle\varepsilon\rangle\varphi\rrbracket}
.

For the other direction, there is a bigger difference due to
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{B^{\prime}}}
allowing more freedom in the placement of conjunction and negation.
In particular, it permits top-level conjunctions and negated conjunctions without
\langle\varepsilon\rangle
in between. But these features do not add distinctive power.

\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{B^{\prime}}}
also allows top-level negation, and this adds distinctive power to the preorders, effectively turning them into equivalence relations.
We do not enforce this and thus our
\mathord{\preceq_{\mathrm{B}}}\neq\mathord{\sim_{\mathrm{B}}}
;
e.g.
\tau\ldotp\!\mathit{a}\preceq_{\mathrm{B}}\tau+\tau\ldotp\!\mathit{a}
, but
\tau+\tau\ldotp\!\mathit{a}\not\preceq_{\mathrm{B}}\tau\ldotp\!\mathit{a}
due to
\langle\varepsilon\rangle{\bigwedge}\{\neg\langle\varepsilon\rangle\langle a\rangle\mathsf{T}\}
.
However, as a distinction by
\neg\varphi
in one direction implies one by
\varphi
in the other, we know that this difference is ironed out once we consider the equivalence
\sim_{\mathrm{B}}
.

3
A Game of Distinguishing Capabilities

This section introduces a game to find out how two states can be distinguished in the silent-step spectrum:
Attacker tries to implicitly construct a distinguishing formula, defender wants to prove that no such formula exists.
The twist is that we use an
energy
game where energies ensure the possible formulas to lie in sublogics along the lines of the previous section.

3.1
Declining Energy Games

Equivalence problems of the strong linear-time–branching-time spectrum can be characterized as multi-dimensional declining energy games with special
\mathtt{min}
-operations between components as outlined in
[
6
]
.
In this subsection, we revisit the definitions we will need in this paper.
For a more detailed presentation—in particular on how to compute attacker and defender winning budgets on this class of games—we refer to
[
6
]
and
[
11
]
.

Definition 3.1

(Energy updates)
.

The set of
energy updates,

\mathbf{Up}
, contains
({u}_{1},\ldots,{u}_{8})\in\mathbf{Up}
where each component
u_{k}
is a symbol of the form

•

u_{k}\in\{-1,0\}
(relative update), or

•

u_{k}=\mathtt{min}_{D}
where
D\subseteq\{1,\ldots,8\}
and
k\in D
(minimum selection update).

Applying an update to an energy,
\mathsf{upd}(e,u)
, where
e=({e}_{1},\ldots,{e}_{8})\in\mathbf{En}_{\infty}
and
u=({u}_{1},\ldots,{u}_{8})\in\mathbf{Up}
, yields a new energy vector
e^{\prime}
where
k
th components
e^{\prime}_{k}\mathrel{\coloneqq}e_{k}+u_{k}
for
u_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}
and
e^{\prime}_{k}\mathrel{\coloneqq}\min_{d\in D}e_{d}
for
u_{k}=\mathtt{min}_{D}
.
Updates that would cause any component to become negative are undefined, i.e.,
\mathsf{upd}
is a partial function.

Example 3.1
.

\mathsf{upd}((2,0,\infty,0,0,0,1,1),(\mathtt{min}_{\{\!1,7\!\}},0,-1,0,0,0,0,-1))
equals
(1,0,\infty,0,0,0,1,0)
.

In the games of
[
6
]
, the attacker wins precisely if they can get the defender stuck without running out of energy.
The energy budgets that suffice for the attacker to win from a game position can be characterized as follows:

Definition 3.3

(Winning budgets)
.

\mathsf{Win}_{\operatorname{a}}^{\mathcal{G}}

\mathcal{G}

3.2
Delaying Observations in the Spectroscopy Energy Game

We begin with the part of the game that adds the concept of “delayed” attack positions to the “strong” spectroscopy game of
[
6
]
.
It matches the black part of the
\mathsf{HML}_{\mathrm{srbb}}
-grammar of
Definition 2.2
.

Figure 4
gives a schematic overview of the game rules, where the game continues from the dashed nodes as from the initial node.
The colors differentiate the layers of following definitions and match the scheme of
Definition 2.2
and
Figure 3
.

Definition 3.4

(Spectroscopy delay game)
.

.
Thus, the attacker wins the game.

The tree of winning moves corresponds to formula
\varphi_{\tau}=\langle\varepsilon\rangle\langle op\rangle\langle\varepsilon\rangle{\bigwedge}\{\neg\langle\varepsilon\rangle\langle b\rangle\mathsf{T}\}
and budget of
Example 2.3
.
This is no coincidence, but rather our core design principle for game moves.
As we will prove in
Section 4
, attacker’s winning moves match distinguishing
\mathsf{HML}_{\mathrm{srbb}}
-formulas and their prices.

Note that the attacker would not win if any component of the starting energy vector were lower.
For example,
e_{\mathrm{T}}=(\infty,0,0,0,0,0,0,0)\notin\mathsf{Win}_{\operatorname{a}}({\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}[}\mathsf{P^{\tau}_{e}},\{\mathsf{P^{\tau}_{\ell}}\}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}]_{\mathtt{a}}^{\smash{\scriptscriptstyle}}})
corresponds to weak trace inclusion,
\mathsf{P^{\tau}_{e}}\preceq_{\mathrm{T}}\mathsf{P^{\tau}_{\ell}}
.

3.3
Covering Stable Failures and Conjunctions

In order to cover “stable” and “stability-respecting” equivalences, we must separately count
stable conjunctions
.

Definition 3.5

(Spectroscopy stability game)
.

The
stability game

\mathcal{G}_{s}^{\mathcal{S}}

extends the delay game
\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathcal{S}}
of
Definition 3.4
by

•

defender stable conjunction positions

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}(}p,Q{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5})_{\mathtt{d}}^{\smash{\scriptscriptstyle s}}}\in G_{\operatorname{d}}
,

where
p\in\mathcal{P}
,
Q\in\mathbf{2}^{\mathcal{P}}
, and three kinds of moves:

•

In principle, we add a move to enter a defender stable conjunction position and a move to leave it, similar to the defender conjunction positions in
Definition 3.4
.

Example 3.3
.

Note that these new rules allow no new (incomparable) wins for the attacker in
Example 3.2
.
Therefore,
stable bisimulation
is another finest preorder (and equivalence) for the example processes
because
e_{\mathrm{SB}}\notin\mathsf{Win}_{\operatorname{a}}({\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}[}\mathsf{P^{\tau}_{e}},\{\mathsf{P^{\tau}_{\ell}}\}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}]_{\mathtt{a}}^{\smash{\scriptscriptstyle}}})
for
\mathcal{G}_{s}
.

3.4
Extending to Branching Bisimulation

One last kind of distinctions is necessary to characterize
branching bisimilarity,
the strongest common abstraction of bisimilarity for systems with silent steps:
its characteristic
branching conjunctions
.

Definition 3.6

(Weak spectroscopy game)
.

weak spectroscopy energy game

\mathcal{G}_{\vartriangle}^{\mathcal{S}}

extends
Definition 3.5
by

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}[}p,Q{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}]_{\mathtt{a}}^{\smash{\scriptscriptstyle}}}
.

Intuitively, the attacker picks a step
p\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\smash{\alpha}}}p^{\prime}
and some
Q_{\alpha}\subseteq Q
that they claim to be inable to immediately simulate this step. For the remaining
Q\setminus Q_{\alpha}
, the attacker claims that these can be dealt with by other (possibly negative) delayed observations.
The defender then chooses which claim to counter.

Example 3.4
.

Consider the CCS processes
\mathit{a}+\tau\ldotp\!\mathit{b}+\mathit{b}
and
\mathit{a}+\tau\ldotp\!\mathit{b}
.
The first process explicitly allows a
\mathit{b}
to happen before deciding against
\mathit{a}
.
To weak bisimilarity, for instance, this is transparent.
To more branching-aware notions, it constitutes a difference.

The two processes can be distinguished as follows in the weak spectroscopy game with energy budget
(1,{\color[rgb]{0,0.7,0.7}1},1,0,0,1,0,0)
:
First, the attacker enters a defender branching position

{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-9.81268pt}{2.52022pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -13.58 3.49)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}(}\mathsf{0},\varnothing{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5})_{\mathtt{d}}^{\smash{\scriptscriptstyle}}}
,
which corresponds to the
\text{\rm(}b\text{\rm)}\mathsf{T}
-observation in the context of a branching conjunction.

Taken together, the attacker wins this game constellation with a strategy that corresponds to the formula
\langle\varepsilon\rangle{\bigwedge}\{\text{\rm(}b\text{\rm)},\langle\varepsilon\rangle\langle a\rangle\}
.

The formula disproves
\eta
-simulation preorder and thus branching bisimilarity.
However, the two processes are (stability-respecting) delay-bisimilar as there are no delay bisimulation formulas to distinguish them.

4
Correctness

We now state in what sense winning energy levels and equivalences coincide in the context of a transition system
\mathcal{S}=(\mathcal{P},\Sigma,\linebreak\mathord{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\smash{}}}})
.

Theorem 4.1

(Correctness)
.

For all
e\in\mathbf{En}_{\infty}
,
p\in\mathcal{P}
,
Q\in\mathbf{2}^{\mathcal{P}}
, the following are equivalent:

1.

There exists a formula
\varphi\in\mathsf{HML}_{\mathrm{srbb}}
with price
\operatorname{\mathsf{expr}}(\varphi)\leq e
that distinguishes
p
from
Q
.

2.

Attacker wins
\mathcal{G}_{\vartriangle}^{\mathcal{S}}
from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}[}p,Q{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}]_{\mathtt{a}}^{\smash{\scriptscriptstyle}}}
with
e
(that is,
e\in\mathsf{Win}_{\operatorname{a}}^{\mathcal{G}_{\vartriangle}^{\mathcal{S}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}[}p,Q{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}]_{\mathtt{a}}^{\smash{\scriptscriptstyle}}})
).

With
Definition 2.7
, this means that,
for a notion of equivalence
N
with coordinate
e_{N}
in
Figure 3
,

p\preceq_{\mathrm{\mathit{N}}}q
precisely if
the defender wins,
e_{N}\notin\mathsf{Win}_{\operatorname{a}}({\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}[}p,\{q\}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}]_{\mathtt{a}}^{\smash{\scriptscriptstyle}}})
.

The proof of the theorem is given through the following three lemmas.
The direction from (1) to (2) is covered by
Lemma 4.1
when combined with the upward-closedness of attacker winning budgets.
From (2) to (1), the link is established through
strategy formulas
by Lemmas
4.2
and
4.3
.
The proofs

can be found on arXiv
[
7
]
and

have also been formalized in an Isabelle/HOL theory.
2
2

2

The formalization can be found on
https://github.com/equivio/silent-step-spectroscopy
.

4.1
Distinguishing formulas imply attacker-winning budgets

Lemma 4.1
.

If
\varphi\in\mathsf{HML}_{\mathrm{srbb}}
distinguishes
p
from
Q
, then
\operatorname{\mathsf{expr}}(\varphi)\in\mathsf{Win}_{\operatorname{a}}({\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}[}p,Q{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}]_{\mathtt{a}}^{\smash{\scriptscriptstyle}}})
.

Proof.

By mutual structural induction on
\varphi
,
\chi
, and
\psi
with respect to the following claims:

1.

If
\varphi\in\mathsf{HML}_{\mathrm{srbb}}
distinguishes
p
from
Q\neq\varnothing
, then
\operatorname{\mathsf{expr}}(\varphi)\in\mathsf{Win}_{\operatorname{a}}({\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}[}p,Q{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}]_{\mathtt{a}}^{\smash{\scriptscriptstyle}}})
;

2.

If
\chi
distinguishes
p
from
Q\neq\varnothing
and
Q
is closed under
\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}
(that is
Q\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}Q
),
then
\operatorname{\mathsf{expr}}^{\varepsilon}(\chi)\in\mathsf{Win}_{\operatorname{a}}({\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}[}p,Q{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}]_{\mathtt{a}}^{\smash{\scriptscriptstyle\varepsilon}}})
;

3.

If
\psi
distinguishes
p
from
q
,
then
\operatorname{\mathsf{expr}}^{\wedge}(\psi)\in\mathsf{Win}_{\operatorname{a}}({\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}[}p,q{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}]_{\mathtt{a}}^{\smash{\scriptscriptstyle\wedge}}})
.

4.

If
{\bigwedge}\Psi
distinguishes
p
from
Q\neq\varnothing
,
then
\operatorname{\mathsf{expr}}^{\varepsilon}({\bigwedge}\Psi)\in\mathsf{Win}_{\operatorname{a}}({\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}(}p,Q{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5})_{\mathtt{d}}^{\smash{\scriptscriptstyle}}})
;

5.

If
{\bigwedge}\{\neg\langle\tau\rangle\mathsf{T}\}\cup\Psi
distinguishes
p
from
Q\neq\varnothing

and all the processes in
Q
are stable,
then
\operatorname{\mathsf{expr}}^{\varepsilon}({\bigwedge}\{\neg\langle\tau\rangle\mathsf{T}\}\cup\Psi)\in\mathsf{Win}_{\operatorname{a}}({\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}(}p,Q{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5})_{\mathtt{d}}^{\smash{\scriptscriptstyle s}}})
;

6.

If
{\bigwedge}\{\text{\rm(}\alpha\text{\rm)}\varphi^{\prime}\}\cup\Psi
distinguishes
p
from
Q
,
then, for any
p\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\smash{\text{\rm(}\alpha\text{\rm)}}}}p^{\prime}\in{\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\rrbracket}
and
Q_{\alpha}=Q\setminus{\llbracket\langle\alpha\rangle\varphi^{\prime}\rrbracket}
,
\operatorname{\mathsf{expr}}^{\varepsilon}({\bigwedge}\{\text{\rm(}\alpha\text{\rm)}\varphi^{\prime}\!\}\linebreak\cup\Psi)\in\mathsf{Win}_{\operatorname{a}}({\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}(}p,\alpha,p^{\prime}\!,\mbox{$Q\setminus Q_{\alpha}$},Q_{\alpha}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5})_{\mathtt{d}}^{\smash{\scriptscriptstyle\eta}}})
.

Full proof in report
[
7
]
.

4.2
Winning attacks imply cheap distinguishing formulas

Definition 4.1

(Strategy formulas)
.

The set of
attacker strategy formulas

\mathsf{Strat}
for a
\mathcal{G}_{\vartriangle}
-position with given energy level
e
is derived from the sets of winning budgets,
\mathsf{Win}_{\operatorname{a}}
, inductively according to the rules in
Figure 6
.

As an example how to read the above rules,
procr
states that if there is a move
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}[}p,Q{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}]_{\mathtt{a}}^{\smash{\scriptscriptstyle\varepsilon}}}\mathrel{\smash{\mathrel{\hbox to10.9pt{\vbox to11.91pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\>\lower-4.88101pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.43pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{{}}{}{}{{{}{}}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.93 0 L 7.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{2.33098pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(-1.0 0.0 0.0 -1.0 3.23 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.93 3.4 C 2.39 1.36 1.2 0.4 0 0 C 1.2 -0.4 2.39 -1.36 2.93 -3.4}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{5.78502pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 8 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.93 3.4 C -2.39 1.36 -1.2 0.4 0 0 C -1.2 -0.4 -2.39 -1.36 -2.93 -3.4}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}[}p^{\prime},Q{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}]_{\mathtt{a}}^{\smash{\scriptscriptstyle\varepsilon}}}

(based on
Definition 3.4
, this must be a procrastination move),
and the strategy formulas of the latter position contain
\chi
, then also the strategy formulas of the former position contain
\chi
.

Lemma 4.2
.

If
e\in\mathsf{Win}_{\operatorname{a}}({\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}[}p,Q{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}]_{\mathtt{a}}^{\smash{\scriptscriptstyle}}})
, then there is
\varphi\in\mathsf{Strat}({\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}[}p,Q{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}]_{\mathtt{a}}^{\smash{\scriptscriptstyle}}},e)
with
\operatorname{\mathsf{expr}}(\varphi)\leq e
.

Proof.

By induction over the structure of
Definition 3.3
.

Full proof in report
[
7
]
.

∎

Lemma 4.3
.

If
\varphi\in\mathsf{Strat}({\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}[}p,Q{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}]_{\mathtt{a}}^{\smash{\scriptscriptstyle}}},e)
,
then
\varphi
distinguishes
p
from
Q
.

Proof.

By induction over the derivation of
~\cdots\in\mathsf{Strat}(g,e)

according to
Definition 4.1
.

Full proof in report
[
7
]
.

∎

Figure 7:
Screenshot of
equiv.io
solving
Example 5.1
.

5
Deciding All Weak Equivalences at Once

The weak spectroscopy energy game enables algorithms to decide all considered behavioral equivalences.
An open-source prototype implementation can be tried out on
https://equiv.io
. Moreover, there is an extension of CAAL (Concurrency Workbench, Aalborg Edition,
[
2
]
) with the entailed algorithm on
https://github.com/equivio/CAAL
.
Both yield the expected output on the finitary examples from
[
24
]
.

The game allows
checking individual equivalences
by instantiating it to start with an energy vector
e_{N}
from
Figure 3
.
The remaining reachability game can be decided with (usually exponential) time and space complexities depending on the selected energy vector.

More generally, one can
decide all equivalences at once
by computing the pareto frontier of attacker budgets
\mathsf{Win}_{\operatorname{a}}({\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}[}p,\{q\}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}]_{\mathtt{a}}^{\smash{\scriptscriptstyle}}})
.
The algorithm of
[
11
]
for multi-weighted games, has space complexity
\mathcal{O}(\lvert\mathord{G}\rvert)
and time complexity
\mathcal{O}(\lvert\mathord{\mathrel{\smash{\mathrel{\hbox to10.35pt{\vbox to9.76pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.17331pt\lower-4.88101pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.43pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{{}}{}

for bounded energies (due to a concrete spectrum),
where
o
is the out-degree of

.
For this paper’s weak spectroscopy game,
\mathcal{G}_{\vartriangle}
,
we have

\lvert\mathord{G_{\vartriangle}}\rvert\in\mathcal{O}(\lvert\mathord{\,\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\smash{}}}}\rvert\cdot 3^{\lvert\mathord{\mathcal{P}}\rvert})

and
\lvert\mathord{\mathrel{\smash{\mathrel{\hbox to10.35pt{\vbox to9.76pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.17331pt\lower-4.88101pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.43pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{{}}{}

,
and also
o_{\vartriangle}\in\mathcal{O}(\lvert\mathord{\,\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\smash{}}}}\rvert\cdot 2^{\lvert\mathord{\mathcal{P}}\rvert})
,
because of the defender branching positions and their surroundings.
This amounts to exponential time complexity.
Clearly, the approach is mostly tailored towards small examples.
But often these are all one needs:

Example 5.1
.

Let us try our initial
Example 2.1
of abstracted processes
(
Figure 7
and
https://equiv.io/#stable-unstable-abstraction
).
The browser tool takes about 100 ms (considering a game of 112 positions) to report that
\mathsf{P_{e}}
and
\mathsf{P_{\ell}}
are stable
and
unstable readiness-equivalent.

\mathsf{P^{\tau}_{e}}
and
\mathsf{P^{\tau}_{\ell}}
on the other hand are stable-bisimilar.
This output immediately tells us that only notions either strictly finer than readiness or coarser than stable bisimilarity can be congruences for abstraction.
In particular, unstable failures, which Gazda et al.
[
20
, Corr. 9]
report to be a congruence for abstraction, cannot be one because the unstable failure formula
\langle\varepsilon\rangle\langle op\rangle\langle\varepsilon\rangle{\bigwedge}\{\neg\langle\varepsilon\rangle\langle a\rangle\mathsf{T}\}
distinguishes
\mathsf{P^{\tau}_{e}}
from
\mathsf{P^{\tau}_{\ell}}
, analogously to
\varphi_{\tau}
of
Example 2.2
.

6
Related Work and Conclusion

This paper provides the first
generalized game characterization
for the spectrum of
“weak” behavioral equivalences
and preorders.
To this end,
Section 2
introduced a new
modal characterization of branching bisimilarity

that can be used to capture the
modal logics of the silent-step spectrum
.
With this perspective, the set of weak equivalence problems becomes just one
quantitative problem
, expressible as one energy game in
Section 3
.

Other
generalized game characterizations
by Chen and Deng
[
12
]
and by us
[
8
,
6
]
have only addressed strong equivalences
or parts of the spectrum
[
29
,
30
]
.
Fahrenberg et al.
[
15
]
treated a quantitative game interpretation for behavioral distances, as well disregarding silent-step notions.
Extending this line of work to account for silent steps in full is necessary for virtually every application.

In the silent-step spectrum, many things are more complicated.
There are
several abstractions of bisimilarity
:
branching,
\eta
, delay and weak bisimilarity, as well as
contrasimilarity, stable bisimilarity and coupled similarity.
We have had to radically depart from their existing games
[
19
,
10
,
9
]
to cover all equivalences.
Depending on
whether stabilization is required
for negated and conjunct observations, each equivalence notion has different weak versions.
Our game characterization is the first to explicitly consider stability-respecting notions, thereby unifying stable equivalences
[
24
]
and unstable ones
[
20
]
.
This unification enables observations about the applicability of (un)stable equivalences as the one in
Example 5.1
.

The
framework of codesigning games and grammars
can also easily be extended to cater for more notions, for instance, divergence-aware ones, or even to combine strong and weak ones in one game.
The connection to energy games enabled us to boost our approach using Brihaye and Goeminne’s recent polynomial decision procedure for multi-weighted games
[
11
]
.

We have added to the rich body of work on
modal characterizations of branching bisimilarity

[
13
,
24
,
16
,
22
,
21
]
.
Continuing
[
8
,
6
]
,
our work participates in a recent trend towards a modal focus for equivalences, also found
in Ford et al.
[
17
]
connecting graded modal logics and monads, and
in Wißmann et al.
[
31
]
as well as Beohar et al.
[
5
]
.
Like Martens and Groote
[
28
]
, we find minimal-depth distinguishing formulas for branching bisimilarity, but we solve the problem for all weak notions at once.

Our main related work, of course, is van Glabbeek’s
linear-time–branching-time spectrum

[
23
,
24
]
.
Up to today, part II on silent steps is available only as “extended abstract” (in two versions!),
while part I has seen a journal version
[
25
]
and refinements by others
[
18
]
.
We hope the present work makes the wisdom on weak equivalences of part II more accessible to tools and humans alike.

Acknowledgments.

We would like to thank Rob van Glabbeek and the EXPRESS/SOS’24 audience for discussing the material with us,
as well as several anonymous referees for pointing out weaknesses in a previous version of this paper.
Special thanks is due to the TU Berlin students Lisa A. Barthel, Leonard M. Hübner, Caroline Lemke, Karl P. P. Mattes, and Lenard Mollenkopf, who validated the present paper in Isabelle/HOL, uncovering and addressing several flaws.
